● I haven't built the skill yet; this is the research and the approach I'd
  suggest. So far only the helper script exists, uncommitted, at
  home/dot_agents/skills/shape-code/scripts/cochange.py. It lists which files
  keep changing together in git history, and it ran correctly on this repo's
  last three commits.

  Are agents actually good at this?

  Only partly. It depends a lot on the kind of problem:

  Kind of problem: Size and structure (class too large, method too long, a class

  that only holds data)
  How well LLMs do: Good, F1 0.80–0.95
  Source: Beyond Strict Rules, 2026
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Kind of problem: Problems that need context (a method that uses another
  object's data more than its own, a subclass ignoring what it inherits, one
  change forcing edits in many files)
  How well LLMs do: Unreliable, F1 0.11–0.71 and below 0.40 for the inheritance
  case
  Source: same study
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Kind of problem: "This method belongs in another class"
  How well LLMs do: Up to 80% of unfiltered suggestions are invalid: invented
  classes, moves that can't work, getters and setters. With mechanical checks
  plus a self-critique step, users rated 83% of suggestions positively.
  Source: MM-assist, ICSME 2025
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Kind of problem: Comments from AI review bots
  How well LLMs do: Developers acted on 1–19% of them, versus 60% of human
  comments. The main reason is noise.
  Source: CRA study
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Kind of problem: Refactorings done by AI
  How well LLMs do: Only 37% were correct without guidance
  Source: CodeScene

  That gives four rules for the skill:

  1. Counts before opinions. A finding must rest on something the main agent can
     recount: how often a method reads its own data versus another object's,
     how many places branch on the same condition, which files co-change in git.
     Anything that is opinion only is never applied automatically.
  2. Recheck every finding before acting on it. The main agent recounts each
     claim and checks that a proposed move can actually work (the target exists,
     can reach what the code needs, no circular dependency). If it doesn't hold
     up, the finding is dropped.
  3. Let the agent judge, not search. In the MM-assist study, LLMs were good at
     deciding where a candidate belongs and bad at finding the candidates.
     Counts and git history pick the candidates; the agent decides where they
     go.
  4. Few findings. At most about 7 suggestions. The rest go into "not pursued"
     with a reason.

  What the other skills do

  - trim-code (triskweline) provides the frame. Two fresh sub-agents that never
    see the author's reasoning, numbered ideas, three lists (applied
    automatically / refactoring of existing code that needs your approval /
    behaviour changes), and a cap on how much existing code a fix may touch.
    Every idea that touches existing code must say what in the diff forced it
    to. Tests run after each fix, and only the fix that broke them is reverted.
  - Matt Pocock's improve-codebase-architecture and codebase-design contribute:
    - the deletion test: delete the class; does the complexity vanish or spread
      to every caller?
    - "one implementation behind an interface is a hypothetical seam; two make
      it real"
    - looking first at the parts of the code that change most often
    - treating recorded architecture decisions (ADRs) as settled
  - Matt's code-review contributes a baseline of 12 classic Fowler code smells,
    with two rules: the repo's documented standards override the baseline, and
    every smell is labelled as a judgement call, never a hard violation.
  - triskweline self-review checks placement ("modularity") as one item on a
    list, with no evidence required. That is exactly what the CRA study shows
    developers ignore.

  None of them separates "how this repo does it" from "what common practice
  says", and none teaches the reader.

  Suggested approach: /shape-code

  Three fresh sub-agents run in parallel on the current diff:

  1. Conventions: for each kind of thing the diff adds (a controller action, a
     form object, an Angular component…), find at least 3 existing examples in
     the repo plus any written rules, and report how the repo does it, with
     counts like "14 of 17 controllers do X". It does not judge whether that is
     good practice.
  2. Placement: for each piece of logic, asks what kind of knowledge it is
     (business rule, input translation, presentation, query, side effect,
     authorization) and which layer owns that kind according to named sources
     (Rails guides, Angular style guide, Fowler, Ousterhout). It works from
     common practice only and ignores the repo's habits, so differences become
     visible.
  3. Boundaries: looks for classes or components to split or merge, and for
     object patterns. It groups each class's methods by the data they share
     (groups that share nothing are split candidates) and applies the
     one-sentence test and the deletion test. A pattern is only suggested when
     at least 2 real variants exist today.

  The main agent compares their answers with this table:

  ┌─────────────────────────────┬───────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │          Situation          │                  Result                   │
  ├─────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ The diff deviates from the  │ Fix the diff to match the repo (clear     │
  │ repo, and the repo's way is │ win)                                      │
  │  sound                      │                                           │
  ├─────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ The repo deviates from      │ The diff stays; noted under "where this   │
  │ common practice, and the    │ repo departs from common practice", with  │
  │ diff follows the repo       │ counts and the harm it causes             │
  ├─────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ The diff follows common     │ You decide: two ways of doing one thing   │
  │ practice where the repo     │ is a cost in itself                       │
  │ doesn't                     │                                           │
  ├─────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ The repo has no pattern for │ Follow common practice; apply it only if  │
  │  this                       │ the evidence is counted                   │
  └─────────────────────────────┴───────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Measures:

  - Main: edits per likely change. Name one realistic future change (say, a new
    discount rule) and count the places you'd edit, before and after. Code that
    changes together should live together.
  - Supporting:
    - own vs. other objects' data accesses
    - groups of methods that share no data
    - git co-change (the script)
    - the same condition branched on in several places
    - dependencies the diff adds
    - concepts gained or removed (from trim-code)
    - what a test must build to reach a rule: a plain object, or a full request
      or TestBed

  I'd leave out cyclomatic complexity and lines of code: they measure size, not
  placement.

  Report: the same lists as trim-code, plus the "where this repo departs"
  section.

  For building your judgement:

  - Every finding names the question to ask, the rule behind the answer, and the
    evidence from your code. For example: "Who owns the data this reads? 7 of 7
    reads are order.*, so a rule lives with the data it reads."
  - The report ends with the 2–4 questions that mattered most in this diff, each
    with this diff's answer.
  - An optional quiz mode shows you the code first and asks where you'd put it,
    then compares your reasoning with the finding.

  Placement guide: one reference file for Rails (rich models, form models, thin
  controllers, side effects in jobs not callbacks, concerns only for a shared
  trait, service objects follow the repo) and one for Angular (smart vs.
  presentational components, HTTP in services, pipes, directives, guards). The
  stack guess comes from your aliases and wiki.

  Overlap with trim-code: shape-code doesn't report dead checks, wrappers or
  size cuts; those stay with trim-code.

  Should I build it this way and commit it, with no apply? It would add
  SKILL.md, two reference files, the script, the two symlink files, and a README
  entry.

  against with several suggestions if these versions would be more maintainable 
  or not and then say which princicples/why/patterns would support that or not

  isolation should in practice require less lines of code/setup to test it      
  properly because we need to test less from integration perspective            
                                                                                
  i think agents are very good at optimzing towards specific goals, but not so  
  good at reasoning randomly about x vs z that leaves too much room for         
  interpreation                                                                 
                                                                                
  ARE THERE OTHER SIMILAR METRICS WE COULD INCLINDe? i also have an issue that

● That's a better design. "Is this in the right place?" turns into "which
  version is cheaper for a concrete future change", and the agent counts instead
  of arguing. It also matches the research: agents are reliable when optimizing
  for a measurable target and unreliable when comparing "X vs Z" freely.

  This approach has a name: scenario-based architecture evaluation (SAAM, Kazman
  et al.). You write likely change requests and count which components each one
  hits. The idea is to make it per diff.

  Revised design: lenses with a number each

  Every lens is a cost function that compares the same set of versions:

  - V0: the diff as written
  - V1–V3: alternative designs that the sub-agents propose to lower the cost

  Lens 1: Change cost (your first lens)

  1. Generate 3 future feature requests that would touch this code in several
     places.
  2. For each request × each version, write the edit list: which files, units
     and lines change.
  3. Count the totals.

  Metrics:
  - Units touched
  - Lines changed
  - Places that must change in lockstep: edits that break something if one is
    forgotten

  Lens 2: Test cost (your second lens)

  1. List the behaviour cases the change must cover; for a discount rule that
     might be 6 cases.
  2. For each version, find the cheapest test level that can verify each case.
  3. Count the setup and assertion lines that test needs.

  Metrics:
  - Test lines per case
  - Cases by level: unit, integration (DB, HTTP, TestBed), end-to-end
  - Cases that need a database, browser or TestBed

  A well-isolated version moves cases down the pyramid and needs fewer lines. A
  version that only shifts tests up the pyramid loses, even if its code looks
  tidier.

  Other countable lenses

  I'd add three of these:

  Lens: Read path (recommended)
  Metric: Files or units to open, and jumps between them, to answer one concrete

  question about the behaviour ("what discount does order X get?")
  What it catches: Code split so finely you can't follow it. This is the
  counterweight to lenses 1–2, which tend to reward ever more extraction.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Lens: Caller knowledge (recommended)
  Metric: Facts a caller must know to use the unit correctly: parameters, call
  order, preconditions, which errors to handle
  What it catches: Shallow interfaces; Pocock's "depth", made countable
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Lens: Second entry point (recommended)
  Metric: Lines needed to run the same behaviour from a job, the console or an
  API endpoint without HTTP or the UI
  What it catches: A rule sitting in too high a layer
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Lens: Removal cost
  Metric: Units and lines to delete the feature cleanly
  What it catches: Feature code smeared across shared code
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Lens: Duplicated knowledge
  Metric: Places that encode the same rule, constant or condition
  What it catches: The same as "bug fixed once, fixed everywhere"
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Lens: Added dependencies
  Metric: Imports and injections the diff adds to each unit
  What it catches: A component or class taking on too much

  I'd leave out cyclomatic complexity, total lines of code and the
  maintainability index. They measure size, not whether the design suits the
  coming change.

  Principles come after the numbers, as predictions

  The order of the steps does the challenging you asked for:

  1. The agents count first.
  2. Only then is each principle or pattern stated as a falsifiable prediction
     for this case. For example: "Single responsibility predicts V2 needs fewer
     edits for S1 than V0."
  3. The counts confirm or refute it.

  The report then says either "Strategy pattern: predicted −3 units for S2,
  measured −1, not worth its 2 extra concepts here" or "Form model: predicted,
  and confirmed for S1 and S3". A principle is never a reason on its own.

  Guarding against gaming the metric

  Risk: Scenarios picked to flatter a favourite design
  Guard: One agent writes the scenarios before any alternative designs exist.
  They are grounded in git history of past changes to these files (the
  co-change script) and in the ticket or requirements. You confirm or replace
  them before counting starts, since you know the domain.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Risk: Line counts gamed by dense code
  Guard: trim-code's rule: shorter but denser doesn't count. Concepts are
  counted
  alongside lines.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Risk: Estimates are noisy
  Guard: A difference counts only past a threshold: at least 2 units, or about
  30% of lines. Anything closer is reported as a tie.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Risk: Lenses rewarding endless extraction
  Guard: The read-path lens pushes back against extraction. A version must not
  get worse on read path to win on change cost.

  How the output changes

  The decision tables are replaced by a score matrix like this one (numbers are
  illustrative):

  V0 as written
  S1 "add a seasonal discount": 4 units / ~35 lines
  S2 "discount via API": 3 / ~40
  S3 "cap per customer": 4 / ~30
  Test lines (6 cases): 120 lines, 5 request specs
  Read path: 4 files
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  V1 rule on Order
  S1 "add a seasonal discount": 1 / ~10
  S2 "discount via API": 1 / ~8
  S3 "cap per customer": 2 / ~15
  Test lines (6 cases): 45 lines, 6 model specs
  Read path: 2 files
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  V2 + DiscountPolicy object
  S1 "add a seasonal discount": 1 / ~12
  S2 "discount via API": 1 / ~8
  S3 "cap per customer": 1 / ~10
  Test lines (6 cases): 50 lines, 6 unit specs
  Read path: 3 files

  After the matrix:

  - Verdict: V1 is a clear win and gets applied. V2 wins only on S3 and costs
    one more file on the read path, so it is up to you.
  - Prediction check: each principle with its predicted versus measured effect.
  - Lists: applied, decision required, and not pursued, as before.

  Two choices for you

  1. How exact the counts are:
     - Estimated (recommended): each agent writes the edit list per scenario and
       version, without real code. It's quick, but counts are approximate.
     - Measured: each version and scenario patch is actually implemented in a
       throwaway git worktree, and the real diff stats are counted. That's 9 or
       more patches per run: precise, but slow and expensive.
     - Hybrid: estimate everything and measure only the winner against V0 before
       applying it.
  2. The scenarios: should the skill always stop for you to confirm them? I'd
     say yes, because good scenarios decide everything, and judging them is the
     domain judgement you want to practise.

❯ ok good only issue the report must be dense and readable and still applicable 
  and valuabe for both small and big changes. what are the most telling ones    
  of other countable lenses?                                                    

● The most telling lenses

  Change cost and test cost stay as the core. Of the extra lenses, only read 
  path measures something those two don't. The others can be folded in, which
  keeps the report small:

  Lens: Read path: files or units to open to answer one concrete question about
  the behaviour
  Keep?: Keep as the 3rd lens
  Why: Change cost and test cost both reward extracting more pieces. Read path
  is
  the only one that penalizes it, so it stops the agent optimizing into a maze
  of tiny classes.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Lens: Duplicated knowledge
  Keep?: Fold into change cost
  Why: A scenario that changes the rule already counts every copy of it.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Lens: Second entry point (job, API, console)
  Keep?: Fold in as a scenario type
  Why: "Run this from a job" becomes one of the scenarios the generator can
  pick.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Lens: Removal cost
  Keep?: Fold in as a scenario type
  Why: Same: "drop this feature" becomes a scenario.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Lens: Caller knowledge
  Keep?: Fold into test cost
  Why: Everything a caller must know shows up as test setup lines.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Lens: Added dependencies
  Keep?: Supporting number only
  Why: Shown in a cell where it explains a result, never scored on its own.

  The scenario generator gets a menu of types: change a rule, add a variant, a
  new entry point, remove the feature, and a new field flowing through all
  layers. It picks the 3 most likely ones from git history and the ticket.

  So there are three lenses: change, test and read.

  Keeping the report dense at any size

  Every report is one matrix and one line per version, nothing more. Depth grows
  with the diff, the report format doesn't.

  ┌────────────────────┬─────────────────┬────────────┬─────────────────────┐
  │     Diff size      │    Scenarios    │  Versions  │     Sub-agents      │
  ├────────────────────┼─────────────────┼────────────┼─────────────────────┤
  │ Small (one unit,   │                 │ V0 + at    │ none, main agent    │
  │ under ~50 lines of │ 1–2             │ most 1     │ alone               │
  │  logic)            │                 │            │                     │
  ├────────────────────┼─────────────────┼────────────┼─────────────────────┤
  │                    │                 │            │ scenario writer,    │
  │ Medium             │ 3               │ V0 + 2     │ then 2 design       │
  │                    │                 │            │ agents              │
  ├────────────────────┼─────────────────┼────────────┼─────────────────────┤
  │ Large              │ 3 per area, at  │ V0 + 2 per │ same, per area      │
  │                    │ most 3 areas    │  area      │                     │
  └────────────────────┴─────────────────┴────────────┴─────────────────────┘

  The rows below V0 show changes against V0, not absolute numbers, so the
  comparison reads at a glance. A dash means the difference is under the noise
  threshold.

  Small change:

  ## Shape: discount badge (1 file)
            S1 new kind   Tests (4 cases)   Read
  V0        2u 18l        60l, 4 TestBed    1
  V1 pipe   −1u −10l      −35l, 4 unit      –
  → V1 applied. Prediction "pure display logic → pipe" ✔ held: −1 unit, tests 
  left TestBed.

  Large change:

  ## Shape: loyalty discount (14 files, 2 areas)

  Area A: discount rule                 Area B: checkout UI
            S1 seasonal  S2 via API  S3 cap/cust  Tests (6)        Read
  V0        4u 35l       3u 40l      4u 30l       120l 5 req       4
  V1 Order  −3u −25l     −2u −32l    −2u −15l     −75l 6 model     −2
  V2 +Policy −3u −23l    −2u −32l    −3u −20l     −70l 6 unit      −1

  ✅ V1 applied: wins all three scenarios, cuts test lines by 60%, and halves 
  the read path.
  🛠️ V2 (your call): beats V1 only on S3 (−1u) and costs +1 file on the read 
  path. Worth it if per-customer caps are coming.
  Predictions: SRP → V2 fewer units ✔ S3 only · Strategy pattern ✘ one kind, +2 
  concepts, no scenario gain
  🚫 Service object: ✘ +1 read, no scenario gain · Concern: ✘ moves lines, 
  changes no count

  Each line ends in a verdict that you act on directly: ✅ applied, 🛠️ your
  decision, 🚫 not pursued. Principles appear only as checked predictions (✔
  held, ✘ didn't). Scenarios are shown to you for confirmation before counting
  starts, so the matrix rows are ones you agreed with.

  Shall I build it this way (counts estimated, the winner measured in a
  throwaway worktree before it's applied) and commit it without applying?

❯ how many lenses would you recommend?                                          

● I'd recommend three lenses: Change, Test and Read. Each one measures something
  the other two can't:

  ┌────────┬─────────────────────┬──────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Lens  │      Measures       │         Why it can't be dropped          │
  ├────────┼─────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │        │ Units and lines     │ It's the main question: is the code      │
  │ Change │ that each future    │ shaped for what comes next?              │
  │        │ request touches     │                                          │
  ├────────┼─────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │        │ Test lines and      │ A version can be easy to change but only │
  │ Test   │ pyramid level       │  testable through requests or TestBed.   │
  │        │ needed to cover the │ Change cost won't show that.             │
  │        │  behaviour          │                                          │
  ├────────┼─────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │        │ Files to open to    │ The other two both reward extracting     │
  │ Read   │ answer one question │ more pieces. Read is the only lens that  │
  │        │  about the          │ punishes it, which stops the agent       │
  │        │ behaviour           │ optimizing into many tiny classes.       │
  └────────┴─────────────────────┴──────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Why not more:

  - The other candidates fold in. Duplicated knowledge, a second entry point and
    removal cost become scenario types. Caller knowledge shows up as test setup
    lines. Added dependencies are a supporting number.
  - The report stays scannable. Each lens is a matrix column. Three scenarios
    plus Test and Read make five columns, which fits one row per version. A
    fourth or fifth lens would turn the matrix into something you skim past.
  - More lenses leave fewer clear winners. A version must win on one lens
    without losing on another. With more lenses, almost every alternative loses
    somewhere, and you're back to "it depends".

  Why not fewer:

  - Change alone pushes toward over-extraction.
  - Change plus Test has no brake against it either, since both like small
    isolated units.
  - Read is that brake.

  If the skill reports clean wins that still feel wrong to you in practice,
  that's the signal a lens is missing. Caller knowledge would be my first
  candidate to bring back as a separate column.

  Shall I continue building with these three?

✻ Sautéed for 7s · done Friday 10:47 PM

❯ i think even for small changes we must ensure that the report is thorough     
  enoguh is this the case?                                                      

● No, not as I'd planned it. For small changes I cut three things that matter
  just as much there:

  Cut for small changes: No fresh sub-agents. The main agent judges alone.
  Why that's a problem: If the main agent wrote the code, it grades its own
  design. This is the bias that trim-code and self-review avoid by using fresh
  agents.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Cut for small changes: 1–2 scenarios, one alternative.
  Why that's a problem: With one scenario, you can't tell "better" from "lucky".

  With one alternative, the obvious other option can be missed.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Cut for small changes: Only the diff's own lines are analysed.
  Why that's a problem: A small change often hurts through where it lands: a
  one-line condition in a controller that repeats a rule living elsewhere, or a
   flag added to an already overloaded component. The diff is small; the cost
  is in the code around it.

  Fix: let thoroughness follow how far the change reaches, not how big it is.
  The report shrinks for small changes; the analysis does not.

  - What's analysed: the diff plus what it plugs into: its callers, the rule it
    extends, and the files git shows changing with it. Scenario counts include
    that existing code.
  - Always the full method: 3 scenarios, written before any alternatives exist,
    then two fresh agents each proposing alternatives, V0 + up to 2 versions,
    and verification.
  - Only truly trivial diffs skip it: a typo, a config value, a pure rename,
    tests only, docs only. They get one line: Nothing to shape: rename only.
  - Sensitive code gets the same depth however small the diff is. That's exactly
    where a 3-line change causes pain.

  Size only changes how many areas get analysed: one for small and medium diffs,
  up to 3 for large ones. It also caps how many tables the report has.

  On cost: two small sub-agents on a small diff adds about a minute and some
  tokens. I think that's worth it in exchange for a report you can trust.

  Shall I build it this way?

● The shape-code skill is committed on main (38daf14). Nothing was applied: it's
  only in the repo for the other machine. It hasn't been run on a real diff
  yet; I only checked that the frontmatter parses and the helper script runs.

  What a run does:
  1. Scenarios: it writes three likely future feature requests from git history
     and the ticket. You confirm or edit them before any counting starts.
  2. Alternatives: two fresh agents propose alternative designs. One moves code
     to the layer or object that owns it; the other splits or merges classes and
     components.
  3. Counts: for the change as written and each alternative, three lenses:
     - Change: units and lines each future request would touch, plus edits that
       must happen together.
     - Test: test lines, and at which pyramid level each behaviour case can be
       tested.
     - Read: files to open to understand the behaviour. This keeps it from
       splitting code into many tiny pieces.
  4. Checks before anything counts as a win:
     - the counts are redone
     - a move must actually be possible
     - a win must be clear, not just a line or two
     - the winning design is built in a throwaway worktree first, to measure
       that the win is real
  5. Apply: clear wins are applied, followed by a cleanup step that removes old
     code paths, leftovers, stale tests and comments.
  6. Your call (🛠️): bigger changes come to you, including ones that change
     behaviour or existing code when the counts make them worth it. Code where
     small mistakes are expensive (money, access, data writes, public APIs, hot
     spots, untested code) is never changed without asking; the skill judges
     which code that is.
  7. Report: one table and one line per decision. Principles appear only as
     predictions the counts confirmed or refuted, and one takeaway line names
     the question to ask yourself next time.

  The report rules ask the skill to compare like this, with the version as
  written in absolute numbers and the alternatives as differences:

  Discount rule      S1         S2         S3         Tests (6 cases)   Read
  V0 as written      4u 35l     3u 40l     4u 30l     120l 0/5/0        4
  V1 rule on Order   −3u −25l   −2u −32l   −2u −15l   −75l 6/0/0        −2
  ✅ V1 rule on Order: wins all three, tests leave the request level.

  Files (under home/dot_agents/skills/shape-code/):
  - SKILL.md: the process and the report format
  - references/lenses.md: how to count, how to pick scenarios, the signals for
    sensitive code, and the research behind each rule
  - references/moves.md: common design moves for Rails and Angular, each with
    what it predicts and what it costs
  - scripts/executable_cochange.py: which files keep changing together in git
    history
  - the Claude and Codex symlink files, and two lines in the skills README
    (shape-code runs before trim-code)

  Two things to know:
  - I wrote it directly rather than through /skill-creator, which this repo's
    dot-manage skill asks for. The backtest described at the end of SKILL.md is
    the real check: run it on older commits and compare its predictions with
    what actually changed later.
  - To install only this skill on the other machine later, run this from the
    repo root:
  chezmoi -S home apply ~/.agents/skills/shape-code ~/.claude/skills/shape-code 
  ~/.codex/skills/shape-code
    It copies the skill to ~/.agents/skills/shape-code and creates the two links
    that make Claude and Codex see it. Nothing else from the repo is deployed.

  Sources:
  - triskweline/skills
  - mattpocock/skills
  - LLM code smell detection study
  - MM-assist: LLM suggestions for moving methods
  - Code review agents in pull requests

● Yes, splitting is good practice, but my split is cut at the wrong place.
  That's the main finding from checking the skill against Anthropic's 
  skill-authoring guide and the installed skill-creator skill.

  Is splitting right?

  The guide treats SKILL.md as an overview. It should stay under 500 lines, with
  material that's only sometimes needed moved into reference files linked
  directly from SKILL.md. Mine already meets the size rule (304 lines) and the
  one-level-deep rule.

  The guide also warns that if Claude reads the same reference file on every
  run, its content probably belongs in SKILL.md. That's the problem with
  lenses.md:

  - The main agent always reads it. Step 1 needs the sensitive-code signals and
    step 4 needs the win thresholds, and both live there.
  - The thresholds exist twice, in SKILL.md and in lenses.md. They will drift
    apart.

  A cleaner cut: SKILL.md holds everything the main agent needs; the reference 
  files are briefings for the sub-agents.

  ┌──────────────────────┬──────────────────┬───────────────────────────────┐
  │         File         │     Read by      │           Contents            │
  ├──────────────────────┼──────────────────┼───────────────────────────────┤
  │                      │                  │ steps, lists, thresholds      │
  │ SKILL.md             │ main agent       │ (once), sensitive-code        │
  │                      │                  │ signals, report format        │
  ├──────────────────────┼──────────────────┼───────────────────────────────┤
  │                      │ scenario and     │ how to pick scenarios and how │
  │ references/lenses.md │ counting         │  to count                     │
  │                      │ sub-agents       │                               │
  ├──────────────────────┼──────────────────┼───────────────────────────────┤
  │ references/moves.md  │ counting         │ design moves with their       │
  │                      │ sub-agents only  │ predictions and costs         │
  └──────────────────────┴──────────────────┴───────────────────────────────┘

  SKILL.md grows to about 330 lines, still well under 500. Each sub-agent gets
  only its own manual, which is also how the skill keeps the scenario writer
  independent of the designs.

  Checklist results

  Check: Name format, description under 1024 characters (it has 817), says what
  and when
  Result: ✔
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Check: Description in third person ("Measures…" rather than "Measure…")
  Result: ✘ fix
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Check: Concrete examples (report template, sub-agent return format)
  Result: ✔
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Check: Feedback loops (verify → drop, measure → tie, tests → revert)
  Result: ✔
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Check: Script handles its own errors
  Result: ✔
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Check: Script defaults explained
  Result: ~ the default of 3 shared commits has no stated reason
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Check: The script runs as written
  Result: ✘ bug. SKILL.md says to run scripts/cochange.py from the repo root,
  but
  that path is relative to the skill's own directory, so it fails. It needs the
   skill's directory in the path.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Check: Sub-agents can find the reference files
  Result: ✘ bug. They receive relative paths but don't know where the skill
  lives. They need absolute paths.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Check: Consistent terms
  Result: ✘ "existing churn" is used three times but never defined
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Check: Table of contents for reference files over 100 lines
  Result: ✘ lenses.md has 128 lines (resolved by the re-cut)
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Check: Only what Claude doesn't already know
  Result: ~ see the two judgement points below
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Check: A diff range can be passed as an argument
  Result: ✘ only uncommitted work or the branch; a commit range you name is
  ignored
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Check: Copyable progress checklist for a 7-step workflow
  Result: ✘ the guide recommends one so steps like verify and cleanup aren't
  skipped
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Check: At least 3 evaluations, tested on Haiku, Sonnet and Opus
  Result: ✘ the biggest gap: none exist, and the skill has never run on a real
  diff

  Two judgement points

  1. The Rails and Angular "usual home" tables in moves.md. Claude already knows
     Rails and Angular conventions. Worse, the tables push exactly the
     well-known answers you want challenged. I'd keep the general moves table,
     where each move has a prediction and a cost (that's the new part), and cut
     the framework tables down to the few contested points: callbacks, concerns,
     service objects. Recommend: trim.
  2. The "Why counts, not opinions" research section. Each sub-agent reading
     lenses.md pays for about 20 lines of citations it doesn't need. SKILL.md
     already states the reason inline at each rule. I'd move the citations to a
     wiki page in this repo. Recommend: move.

  Proposed fixes

  - Clear fixes: the two path bugs, the third-person description, defining
    existing churn, the diff-range argument, the progress checklist, and the
    reason for the script's default.
  - Restructure: the SKILL.md / sub-agent briefing re-cut.
  - The two judgement points above.

  Evaluations stay open: they need real Rails and Angular diffs, so they belong
  on the other machine. There, skill-creator's eval loop plus the backtest
  described in the skill would be the real test.

  Shall I apply all of these and commit on main?

      107 -                                                              
      108 -## Why counts, not opinions                                   
      109 -                                                              
      110 -These rules come from studies of how well language models judg
          -e design:                                                     
      111 -                                                              
      112 -- LLMs detect size and structure problems well (large class, l
          -ong method: F1 0.80–0.95),                                    
      113 -  but context-dependent ones poorly (feature envy F1 0.11–0.71
          -, refused bequest below                                       
      114 -  0.40, shotgun surgery at most 0.63). Hence: counts the main 
          -agent can redo, and git                                       
      115 -  history for change coupling.                                
      116 -  [Beyond Strict Rules, 2026](https://arxiv.org/html/2601.0987
          -3v1)                                                          
      117 -- Up to 80% of unfiltered LLM suggestions to move a method els
          -ewhere were invalid: classes                                  
      118 -  that don't exist, moves that can't work. Mechanical checks p
          -lus self-critique made them                                   
      119 -  useful; LLMs were better at judging a target than at finding
          - what to move. Hence step                                     
      120 -  4's verification. [MM-assist, ICSME 2025](https://arxiv.org/
          -html/2503.20934v2)                                            
      121 -- Developers acted on 1–19% of review bots' comments, against 
          -60% of human comments,                                        
      122 -  mostly because of noise. Hence the caps and the one-line rep
          -ort.                                                          
      123 -  [Code review agents in pull requests](https://arxiv.org/html
          -/2604.03196v1)                                                
      124 -- AI refactorings were correct in 37% of cases without guidanc
          -e. Hence pin-down tests,                                      
      125 -  the measured check and the test run.                        
      126 -  [CodeScene, Refactoring vs Refuctoring](https://codescene.co
          -m/hubfs/whitepapers/Refactoring-vs-Refuctoring-Advancing-the-s
          -tate-of-AI-automated-code-improvements.pdf)                   
      127 -- Counting how many components a likely change touches is scen
          -ario-based architecture                                       
      128 -  evaluation (SAAM, Kazman et al. 1994), applied here to a sin
          -gle diff.

       5  lenses, or the prediction failed for this code. The repo's own

       8 -## General moves                                               
       9 -                                                               
       8  | Move | Predicts | Costs | Signal in the code |
       9  |---|---|---|---|
      10  | Put a rule with the data it reads | fewer units for "change t
          he rule"; unit tests instead of request or TestBed tests | can 
          bloat the data owner; watch Read | most of the code's reads go 
          to one other object |
     ...
      16  | One map or polymorphism instead of repeated branches | "add a
           variant" touches one place | +1 concept per variant class | th
          e same `if` or `case` on the same value in 2+ places |
      17  | A seam (port, adapter, injected collaborator) | unit tests wi
          thout the outside system | +1 interface | only pays off with tw
          o real implementations, production and test; with one it is ind
          irection |
      18  | A strategy, registry or plugin point | "add a variant" withou
          t touching existing code | +2 concepts | pays off only with 2+ 
          variants today, or a scenario confirmed by history |
      21 -                                                               
      22 -## Rails                                                       
      23 -                                                               
      24 -| Kind of code | Usual home | Notes |                          
      25 -|---|---|---|                                                  
      26 -| A business rule, invariant or derived value | the model it re
         -ads from (models need not be ActiveRecord: plain classes in `ap
         -p/models` are fine) | |                                        
      27 -| One user interaction that spans models, or has virtual attrib
         -utes | a form model (ActiveModel, or ActiveType where the repo 
         -uses it) | keeps interaction-specific validations and callbacks
         - off the core model |                                          
      28 -| Translating HTTP to calls and back | the controller: params, 
         -one call into the model layer, a render or redirect | a branch 
         -on domain state in a controller is a rule in the wrong layer | 
      29 -| A named, reused query | a scope; a query object if it is comp
         -lex | |                                                        
      30 -| Display formatting | a helper, presenter or view component | 
         -no queries and no rules in views |                             
      31 -| Mail, HTTP calls, indexing | a job or mailer, triggered from 
         -the use case | `after_save` callbacks are for the record's own 
         -data integrity; side effects there also fire from the console, 
         -imports and tests |

  Each run sees only the history up to the feature commit. The later commits
  stay outside the copies, as the answer key for grading.

  Assertions: each run is graded on these eight yes/no checks.

  1. Predicted real changes: at least 2 of its anticipated future changes match
     the kind of change a real later commit made.
  2. The preferred design would have helped: the alternative it applied or
     recommended puts in one place code that a real later commit had to edit
     across several files.
  3. Compares in numbers: the change as written versus at least one concrete
     alternative, on change, test and read cost.
  4. Every decision cites a count.
  5. Principles only as checked claims, never as the sole reason.
  6. Scannable: the report has at most 30 non-empty lines.
  7. Applied edits avoid migrations, schema, authentication and session code.
  8. Applied at least one change, cleanly: no shims, leftovers or stale
     references.

  Checks 1 and 2 are the actual backtest. Checks 3–6 test the report format,
  which the baseline isn't asked for, so they will mostly separate the arms by
  design. Checks 7 and 8 test the applied edits.

  When the runs finish, I'll save each run's timing, grade it against the answer
  key, aggregate the benchmark, and open skill-creator's viewer for you.

● The first evaluation loop is complete. On skill-creator's benchmark,
  shape-code scored 96% with the skill against 52% without on three real
  commits, but that headline overstates it. The viewer should be open in your
  browser at http://127.0.0.1:3117: "Outputs" lets you click through each report
  and leave feedback, and "Benchmark" has the numbers. Click "Submit All
  Reviews" when you're done.

  What was run: three real feature commits:

  - lobsters: story saving (Rails)
  - maybe: accounts list (Rails with Hotwire)
  - ghostfolio: tags on holdings (Angular with NestJS)

  Each was run once with the skill and once without, on Opus as headless Claude
  Code sessions. Each run only saw history up to the commit. Fresh grader agents
  then scored it against what the maintainers actually did over the next 18
  months.

  Results per assertion (✔/✘ for lobsters, maybe, ghostfolio):

  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────┬─────────┬─────────┐
  │                     Assertion                      │  With   │ Without │
  │                                                    │  skill  │         │
  ├────────────────────────────────────────────────────┼─────────┼─────────┤
  │ Predicted real future changes                      │ ✘✔✔     │ ✔✔✔     │
  ├────────────────────────────────────────────────────┼─────────┼─────────┤
  │ Design verdict matches what really happened        │ ✔✔✔     │ ✘✔✔     │
  ├────────────────────────────────────────────────────┼─────────┼─────────┤
  │ Found bugs the maintainers later fixed             │ ✔✔✔     │ ✔✔✔     │
  ├────────────────────────────────────────────────────┼─────────┼─────────┤
  │ Compares designs in numbers / cites counts /       │ ✔ on    │ ✘ on    │
  │ checks principles / report ≤30 lines               │ all     │ all     │
  ├────────────────────────────────────────────────────┼─────────┼─────────┤
  │ Stayed out of sensitive code / applied changes     │ ✔ on    │ ✔ on    │
  │ cleanly                                            │ all     │ all     │
  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────┴─────────┴─────────┘

  The four method-and-format rows test whether the skill's method was followed.
  The baseline was never asked for them, so they inflate the headline score. The
  assertions that check against real history are close to even:

  ┌────────────────────────┬────────────┬─────────┐
  │ Real-history assertion │ With skill │ Without │
  ├────────────────────────┼────────────┼─────────┤
  │ Design verdict         │ 3/3        │ 2/3     │
  ├────────────────────────┼────────────┼─────────┤
  │ Predicted changes      │ 2/3        │ 3/3     │
  ├────────────────────────┼────────────┼─────────┤
  │ Real bugs found        │ 3/3        │ 3/3     │
  └────────────────────────┴────────────┴─────────┘

  What the skill does well:
  - Short reports: 17–24 lines, against 58–67 for the baseline.
  - Correct verdicts in all three evals:
    - In maybe, its top recommendation is what the maintainers built five days
      later.
    - In ghostfolio, its shared tags-selector matches the real refactor almost
      exactly: same path, same inputs and output.
    - In lobsters, "keep it, it mirrors hide-story" held up.
  - No edits that changed behaviour. Two baseline runs changed behaviour while
    calling it cleanup, and the maybe baseline's structural changes were
    discarded upstream later.

  Problems found, for iteration 2:
  1. It applies almost nothing: 0, 0 and 1 line, even certain bugs the
     maintainers later fixed, like the currency display in maybe and the RSS
     token in lobsters. The sensitive-code rule plus a strict win threshold
     sends nearly everything to "Your call". That contradicts "fix clear wins
     right away".
  2. The scenarios miss what actually comes next. In lobsters none of the three
     happened. The real follow-ups were bug fixes within hours and refactors
     within weeks, which the scenario menu doesn't cover.
  3. "Follow the repo" blocked good designs twice. The maintainers later adopted
     alternatives the skill had rejected as "a second way next to the
     siblings".
  4. It costs more: about 2.3× the tokens, $2.59 against $0.97 per run, and 5 to
     26 minutes.
  5. The evals need fixes:
     - drop or down-weight the format assertions
     - add a precision check on scenarios
     - credit applied changes that history later confirmed

  Two caveats: this is one run per configuration on three commits, and
  ghostfolio's 9/9 rests on three generous grader readings.

  Everything lives in ~/.cache/shape-code-evals/. A full iteration costs about
  $10–12 of usage when run one pair at a time. Once you've left feedback in the
  viewer, tell me and I'll revise the skill starting with problems 1–3, then run
  iteration 2 against these results.

❯ is the skill bad then?                                                        

● No, it's not bad. But on this evidence it isn't clearly better than plain
  Claude at the substance. What it does reliably is change the form of the
  review. That's a real gain for what you wanted, though not the one the 96%
  suggests.

  Where it clearly helps you

  - It answers your actual question. You wanted "is everything in the right
    place?" settled by numbers, not by opinion. The skill did that every time;
    the baseline never did. The baseline argued with phrases like "overkill" and
    "clearer", which is exactly the "X vs Z" reasoning you distrust.
  - Its verdicts were right in all three evals, including the one that's hardest
    for a reviewer: "keep it as written". The baseline's top call was wrong
    once.
  - It's careful. Two baseline runs changed behaviour while presenting it as
    cleanup, and one baseline's structural edits were later thrown away
    upstream. The skill made no edit like that.
  - The reports are readable: under 25 lines against about 60. You said you'd
    stop using a skill that overwhelms you, and the baseline reports would.

  Where it falls short

  - It doesn't fix things, which was half your request. As written, "never touch
    sensitive code" and "only apply clear wins" combine into "apply nothing",
    so you get a decision list where you wanted fixes.
  - The scenario step, the core of the method, is the weakest part. It works
    when the next changes are features, as in maybe, and fails when they're bug
    fixes and refactors, as in lobsters. That's fixable: build scenarios from
    what typically follows a feature in this repo's history, not from imagined
    feature requests.
  - It costs about 2.5× as much.
  - On finding bugs and anticipating changes, plain Claude was just as good. The
    skill's advantage is judgement and discipline, not discovery.

  How sure is this? Only somewhat. It's three commits, one run each, so a single
  grader's call can swing a result.

  The two biggest problems are concrete rules in the skill, not flaws in the
  idea:

  - Applying: let certain, local bug fixes and clear wins be applied even near
    sensitive code when the edit itself is small and checked.
  - Scenarios: add follow-up fixes and refactors as scenario types, drawn from
    git history.

  Fixing those two and re-running is the cheapest way to find out whether the
  method holds up. Should I do that as iteration 2?

✻ Sautéed for 7s · done Saturday 9:18 AM

● 2 background shell command tasks didn't finish before the previous session
ended. Task ids: b83tmy39n, btphzj0am.

❯ i don't mind if it doesnt fix too many things it's mainly for analysis for    
  me, i can still tell which ones to apply. for me it's mainly to make the code 
  maintainable for long term, not so much for bug fixes, i.e. when we add x or  
  y, how much hustle will it be? i think a refactor should exactly serve this   
  purpose, right? wdyt?                                                         

● Yes, I agree. That's the textbook justification for a refactor. Kent Beck's
  line is "make the change easy, then make the easy change", and Fowler calls it
  preparatory refactoring. A refactor earns its cost by making the next likely
  changes cheaper. It doesn't earn it by looking cleaner.

  One limit: a refactor is only as good as the guess about what comes next.
  Refactoring for changes that never come is speculative generality, and it
  makes code harder to maintain, not easier. The evals showed exactly that this
  guess is the weak point:

  - maybe: the predicted changes really happened, and the skill's recommendation
    matched what the maintainers built five days later.
  - lobsters: none of the predicted changes happened. The skill rightly
    recommended no refactor, but by luck: the counts came out even, not because
    it saw what was coming.

  So for your purpose, the scenarios matter most, more than the counting or the
  fixing.

  What I'd change for iteration 2, given your answer:

  1. Make it analysis-first. Report by default, and apply only when you say
     apply V2. That drops the expensive measuring step and the sensitive-code
     worry, and most of the cost with them.
  2. Ground the scenarios better. I'm dropping my bug-fix idea, since you don't
     care about that.
     - From history: take kinds of change from what followed similar features in
       this repo's git history, not only from imagination.
     - Likelihood: label each scenario "seen 4 times in history", "from the
       ticket" or "guess", and weigh the counts by it.
     - Your input stays the strongest signal. When you confirm the scenarios
       ("we'll add multi-currency next quarter"), you're supplying the roadmap
       that no git history contains.
  3. Prefer robust wins. A refactor should pay off across several plausible
     scenarios. If it only wins for one guessed scenario, the skill should say
     so and lean toward the simplest design. Code that's easy to delete or
     change is the safe default when the future is unclear.
  4. Fix the evals.
     - Drop the four format checks that the baseline was never asked for.
     - Measure how many predicted scenarios really happened.
     - Measure whether the recommended design would have made the real later 
       commits smaller.

     The second measurement is the one that tests your "how much hassle?"
     question directly.

  Cost: iteration 2 would be about $10–12 of usage, run one pair at a time as
  before.

  Shall I go ahead?

✻ Crunched for 29s · done Saturday 1:51 PM

❯ yes it's more about making the code robust for anything that might come and   
  less about speculative refacotrings, maybe that part should even be kind of   
  optional because i don't want it too speculate too much about this and that,  
  because that might require a very specifci shape, but i rather want it to be  
  a shape that is robust for whatever comes next,                               

● That changes the core of the skill, and for the better: measure robustness for
  whatever comes next, rather than fitness for three guessed futures.

  You can measure robustness without guessing. Use Parnas's classic criterion
  for good modules: every design decision that might change sits in exactly one
  place. That replaces the imagined scenarios with something exhaustive and
  non-speculative: change each decision the code already makes, once, and count 
  what breaks.

  The new main lens: places per decision. The skill lists every decision the
  diff encodes:

  - a business rule ("accounts count as active when…")
  - a data field and where it flows
  - a display format
  - a permission check
  - an external API or library
  - a threshold or constant

  For each one it counts the units that would have to change if that decision
  changed. The robust shape is the one where most decisions live in one place.

  ┌─────────────────────────────┬────────────────────┬─────┐
  │    Decision in the diff     │   V0 as written    │ V1  │
  ├─────────────────────────────┼────────────────────┼─────┤
  │ what "active account" means │ 4 places           │ 1   │
  ├─────────────────────────────┼────────────────────┼─────┤
  │ saved-list order            │ 1                  │ 1   │
  ├─────────────────────────────┼────────────────────┼─────┤
  │ the tags field              │ 6 places, lockstep │ 3   │
  └─────────────────────────────┴────────────────────┴─────┘

  Why this fits what you want:

  - No speculation. It only asks what happens when something the code already 
    decides changes. Every future change touches some existing decision, so this
    covers whatever comes.
  - No specific shape required. It penalizes the same knowledge spread across
    places and tight coupling. It never demands an abstraction for a guessed
    variant. A strategy pattern or plugin point only wins if it reduces places
    for decisions that exist today.
  - It keeps its counterweights. The Test lens (can each rule be tested in
    isolation?) and the Read lens (how many files to open to understand it?)
    stay, so "robust" can't turn into a maze of tiny classes.

  Scenarios become optional. They're used only when you name upcoming work
  ("we'll add multi-currency next"), or as a tie-breaker the skill marks as a
  guess. The report never rests on them.

  Everything else from before stays:

  - analysis-first, applying only on apply V2
  - the dense matrix, at one row per decision instead of one column per scenario
  - principles as checked predictions
  - the takeaway line

  The backtest gets sharper: did the shape with fewer places per decision
  actually make the real later commits smaller? That checks whether the metric
  predicts what you care about.

  Shall I rebuild the skill around this and run iteration 2?

✻ Baked for 24s · done Saturday 1:54 PM

❯ ok let's just quickly see how this would for example apply to good rails or   
  angular architchture                                                          
● Here's how places-per-decision plays out on standard Rails and Angular
  architecture advice. In short, each well-known pattern is a tool for putting a
  decision in one place. The metric shows when it does that, and when it only
  adds files.

  Rails

  Advice: Fat model, skinny controller
  Decision it's about: "what counts as an active account"
  When it wins: The rule is repeated in a controller, a view, a mailer and a
  query. A scope :active plus active? on the model takes it from 4  places to 
  1.
  When it doesn't: The rule is used in one controller action only. Moving it
  gives 1 → 1, so there's no robustness gain. Moving it can still win on Test
  (a model spec instead of a request spec), which the Test lens counts
  separately.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Advice: Service objects
  Decision it's about: "what happens when an order is placed" (charge, email,
  log)
  When it wins: The controller and a nightly job both run those steps. One
  PlaceOrder takes it from 2 places to 1.
  When it doesn't: It wraps a single Order.create. That's still 1 place, plus
  one
  more file to read. The metric says no, which challenges the habit of reaching
   for a service by default.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Advice: Callbacks vs explicit calls
  Decision it's about: "when the welcome email goes out"
  When it wins: There's one entry point. An after_create callback is 1 place,
  and
  that's fine.
  When it doesn't: Imports and admin tools must skip it. Every new entry point
  then needs a skip_email flag, so the decision leaks into N  places. Calling
  it explicitly from the signup use case keeps it at 1.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Advice: Form objects
  Decision it's about: "what signup requires"
  When it wins: The rules are scattered across User and Company as validates 
  ...,
   if: :signing_up. That condition is one decision in many places; a Signup
  form object takes it to 1.
  When it doesn't: A plain one-model form gains nothing.

  Angular

  Advice: HTTP in services, not components
  Decision it's about: "endpoint and response shape"
  When it wins: Three components call HttpClient for holdings. One
  HoldingsService takes it from 3 places to 1.
  When it doesn't: One component calls it once: 1 → 1. The gain is only on Test
  (no HTTP mock in a TestBed), so the skill reports it there.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Advice: Smart vs presentational components
  Decision it's about: "how a holding's tags are shown and edited"
  When it wins: Two dialogs each build their own tag editor. A shared
  presentational TagsSelector takes it from 2 places to 1. That's exactly the
  refactor ghostfolio really did, and the one the skill recommended in the
  eval.
  When it doesn't: Splitting a component only one screen uses adds a file
  without
  lowering any count.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Advice: A store (NgRx, signals)
  Decision it's about: "the current portfolio filter"
  When it wins: Four components read and derive it themselves. One store takes
  it
  from 4 places to 1.
  When it doesn't: One component uses it. A store then spreads one decision over

  an action, a reducer, a selector and an effect: 1 → 4. The metric penalizes
  it, which says what experienced Angular devs say ("you don't need NgRx yet"),
   with a number behind it.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Advice: Pipes for formatting
  Decision it's about: "how money is displayed"
  When it wins: Copies of currency:... with arguments in six templates. A money
  pipe takes it from 6 places to 1.
  When it doesn't: One template uses it once.

  What this means for the skill

  - No pattern is good or bad in itself. The same service object, store or form
    object is right in one diff and wrong in another. The count decides, so you
    don't get "always use services" dogma.
  - It's a judgement you can learn. The question to ask yourself: "if this 
    decision changed, how many places would I edit?" More than one means the
    decision needs a home. Exactly one means leave it, however untidy it looks.
  - The counterweights still apply. A version that gets to 1 place by adding a
    layer you must open on every read pays for it on the Read lens. One that
    gets there by pushing tests up to request specs pays on the Test lens.

  Shall I rebuild the skill around this and run iteration 2?

✻ Baked for 28s · done Saturday 1:56 PM

❯ ok i like the lense let's now compare it with the test and readable lens      

● The three lenses each answer a different question about the same design, so
  they often agree but sometimes pull apart. When they pull apart, that's where
  the real decisions are.

  What each lens measures

  Lens: Decisions (robustness)
  Question: If one thing the code decides changes, how many places change?
  Count: places per decision, and edits that must happen together
  Rewards: each decision in one home
  Can be gamed by: extracting a new layer for every decision
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Lens: Test
  Question: How cheaply can each behaviour be verified?
  Count: test lines per case, and cases needing a DB, browser or TestBed
  Rewards: rules in plain objects, thin wiring
  Can be gamed by: splitting code into pieces only tests use
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Lens: Read
  Question: How many files to open to understand one behaviour?
  Count: files and jumps from entry point to answer
  Rewards: code that belongs together, together
  Can be gamed by: stuffing everything into one big class

  Where they agree (most of the time)

  Rails: rule in a controller, repeated in a view and a mailer, moved to 
  Order#discount.

  ┌─────┬───────────┬────────────────────────────┬─────────┐
  │     │ Decisions │            Test            │  Read   │
  ├─────┼───────────┼────────────────────────────┼─────────┤
  │ V0  │ 3 places  │ 5 request specs, 120 lines │ 3 files │
  ├─────┼───────────┼────────────────────────────┼─────────┤
  │ V1  │ 1         │ 5 model specs, 45 lines    │ 1 file  │
  └─────┴───────────┴────────────────────────────┴─────────┘

  All three improve. A clear win.

  Angular: two dialogs with their own tag editor, merged into a shared 
  TagsSelector.

  ┌─────┬───────────┬───────────────────────────┬─────────────────────────┐
  │     │ Decisions │           Test            │          Read           │
  ├─────┼───────────┼───────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────┤
  │ V0  │ 2 places  │ 2 TestBed suites          │ 2 files                 │
  ├─────┼───────────┼───────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────┤
  │ V1  │ 1         │ 1 suite with plain inputs │ 1 file plus 1 component │
  └─────┴───────────┴───────────────────────────┴─────────────────────────┘

  Mostly better. Read is a tie, because a new file sits next to fewer
  duplicates.

  When the lenses agree, the pattern is earning its keep.

  Where they disagree

  Case: Service object around one model call
  Decisions: 1 → 1
  Test: –
  Read: +1 file
  Who's right: Read says no, nothing else wins: don't.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Case: Extract a rule used once into a plain class, so it can be tested without

  Rails
  Decisions: 1 → 1
  Test: 6 request specs → 6 unit specs
  Read: +1 file
  Who's right: Test against Read. Worth it when there are many cases, as with a
  pricing rule; not for one if.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Case: NgRx store for state one component uses
  Decisions: 1 → 4
  Test: about the same
  Read: +3 files
  Who's right: All against it: don't.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Case: Store for a filter four components derive themselves
  Decisions: 4 → 1
  Test: 4 suites → 1 reducer spec
  Read: +2 files
  Who's right: Decisions and Test against Read. Worth it.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Case: Splitting a big component by region
  Decisions: –
  Test: smaller setup per suite
  Read: +2 files
  Who's right: Test against Read. Only if the regions change for different
  reasons; otherwise keep it.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Case: One generic MarkStory model for hide and save (lobsters)
  Decisions: 2 → 1 for "how a mark works"
  Test: –
  Read: +1 concept, metaprogramming
  Who's right: Decisions against Read. It only pays when a third kind of mark
  arrives. That's exactly what the skill concluded in the eval.

  How to decide when they conflict

  The skill would use these rules:

  1. Decisions lead. Robustness is the goal; Test and Read are the guardrails.
  2. A version must not be worse on Read by more than 1 file for each decision 
     it saves. That stops extraction for its own sake.
  3. A Test win alone justifies a change only when it moves many cases down the 
     pyramid (roughly 5 or more). One case doesn't pay for a new file.
  4. When the lenses disagree and none of these rules settles it, it's your 
     call. The report shows the trade-off in one line, for example +1 Read file 
     buys 6 cases at unit level, and you decide.

  In the report, the decisions table gets two columns for the Test and Read
  totals per version, so each trade-off shows at a glance:

                     what "active" means  tags field  saved order   Tests       
    Read
  V0 as written      4                    6 lockstep  1             120l 0/5/0  
    4
  V1 rule on Account 1                    6 lockstep  1             −75l 5/0/0  
    −2
  V2 + TagsSelector  1                    3           1             −80l 5/0/0  
    −1

  Shall I rebuild the skill around these three lenses with these rules, and run
  iteration 2?

✻ Churned for 17s · done Saturday 1:59 PM

❯ is there another lense that might be worth adding?                            

● I'd keep three lenses. The candidates worth having fit into the Decisions lens
  as additional kinds of decision, so they add rows, not columns.

  Candidate lens: Removal cost: lines and units to delete the feature cleanly
  What it would catch: a feature tangled into shared code
  Is it new?: Folds in. "This feature exists" is a decision. Add it as a row: if

  it changed (removed), how many places change?
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Candidate lens: Blast radius: how many units break when one unit's interface
  changes
  What it would catch: tight coupling, wide interfaces
  Is it new?: Folds in. A unit's interface is a decision too. Add a row for each

  interface the diff creates or widens.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Candidate lens: Library and framework lock-in: how much code knows about an
  outside library
  What it would catch: an HTTP client, gem or chart library used all over
  Is it new?: Folds in. "We use library X" is a decision; count the places that
  touch it directly.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Candidate lens: Caller knowledge: facts a caller must know (parameters, call
  order, preconditions)
  What it would catch: shallow interfaces
  Is it new?: Mostly folds into Test, since every fact a caller must know
  becomes
  test setup lines.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Candidate lens: Invalid states: combinations of flags or fields the code
  allows
  but that make no sense (three booleans give 8 combinations, 4 valid)
  What it would catch: bugs that come with change, since each new state
  multiplies the cases
  Is it new?: The only distinct one. It folds into Test only partly.

  Recommendation:

  - Widen the Decisions lens. The skill lists decisions in five kinds: rules,
    data fields, formats and permissions, plus feature existence, interfaces and
    outside libraries. That covers removal cost, blast radius and lock-in with
    no new column, and keeps the report as dense as it is.
  - Invalid states as a supporting number only. It's shown next to the counts
    when the diff introduces flags or optional fields that describe one state,
    for example 3 flags: 4 of 8 combinations valid. It's not a lens of its own,
    because it rarely applies, and a column that's empty most of the time adds
    noise.

  A fourth full lens would cost more than it brings. It would fill the matrix,
  and lenses that overlap make the "who wins?" rules muddy again. You said
  earlier that agents do well when optimizing toward clear goals and badly when
  weighing x against z; three non-overlapping lenses keep it that way.

  Shall I rebuild the skill this way, with Decisions (wider), Test, Read and
  invalid states as a supporting number, and run iteration 2?

❯ i mean for test lense we should also account for how much is involved, for    
  rails the involvement it is pretty well abstracted away, but in angular

● Agreed, and that's a sharp point. Test level alone hides it: an Angular "unit"
  test with a TestBed full of providers and mocks can cost more than a Rails
  request spec with two factories. So the Test lens will count what each test 
  has to set up, not just its level.

  Setup per case is the collaborators a test must build or fake for one
  behaviour case:

  - Rails: records or factories created, a logged-in user, stubbed services,
    mail or job assertions
  - Angular: TestBed imports, providers, mocks or spies, the HTTP testing
    controller, fixture and change-detection plumbing

  That makes the framework weight visible in one framework-neutral number. A
  pricing rule in a plain function costs 0–1 per case. The same rule inside a
  component typically costs 4–6 under TestBed, and maybe 2 inside a Rails model
  spec.

  Rebuilding the skill now:

  - Decisions: the main lens, now including feature existence, interfaces and
    outside libraries
  - Test: setup per case plus test lines
  - Read: as before
  - Invalid states: a supporting number only
  - Scenarios: optional, used only when you name upcoming work
  - Applying: analysis-first, nothing applied without apply V2

  Read 1 file (ctrl+o to expand)

  report's one-line outcomes will be called verdicts and the brief's "decisions
  already made" will be choices the human already made. Now writing the new
  SKILL.md.

          +r good the analysis behind                                    
       36 +it. The skill analyzes; it changes code only when the human as
          +ks.

          -hing a unit counts it once,

       68  ## The three lenses
       69  
       70  Each lens is a count. The sub-agents count by [references/lens
           es.md](references/lenses.md).
       71  
       60 -1. **Change.** For each scenario: the units touched, the lines
          - of logic changed, and the                                    
       61 -   lockstep edits (edits that break something when one of them
          - is forgotten).                                               
       62 -2. **Test.** The behavior cases the diff must cover, listed on
          -ce. For each version: the                                     
       63 -   cheapest test level that verifies each case, and the lines 
          -of setup and assertion it                                     
       64 -   needs. Good isolation moves cases down the test pyramid and
          - needs fewer lines.                                           
       72 +1. **Decisions** (the main lens). Every decision in the reach,
          + with its places in each                                      
       73 +   version, and which of them are lockstep.                   
       74 +2. **Test.** The behavior cases, listed once. For each version
          +: what a test of each case                                    
       75 +   has to set up (records, factories, logins, providers, mocks
          +, spies, HTTP fakes,                                          
       76 +   fixtures) and its lines. Setup per case makes framework wei
          +ght visible: a rule in a                                      
       77 +   plain function needs almost none, the same rule in a compon
          +ent under a test bed needs                                    
       78 +   a lot.                                                     
       79  3. **Read.** For one concrete question about the behavior ("wh
           at discount does order 42
       66 -   get?"): the files a reader opens to answer it. Change and T
          -est reward extracting more                                    
       67 -   units; Read is the counterweight against a maze of tiny one
          -s.                                                            
       80 +   get?"): the files a reader opens to answer it.             
       81  
       69 -**A version beats V0** when it is better on Change by at least
          - 2 units or about 30% of                                      
       70 -the lines summed over the scenarios, or better on Test by abou
          -t 30% of the lines, and no                                    
       71 -worse on any lens beyond noise. One more file on Read, or one 
          -more lockstep edit, is                                        
       72 -worse. Smaller differences are ties: estimates are rough, and 
          -a tie is reported as one.                                     
       82 +A supporting number, shown only when it applies: **invalid sta
          +tes**, when the diff adds                                     
       83 +flags or optional fields that together describe one state ("3 
          +flags: 4 of 8 combinations                                    
       84 +valid").                                                      
       85  
       74 -**Counts can be gamed.** Shorter but denser doesn't count: sev
          -eral statements packed                                        
       75 -into a line, long call chains, clever tricks, metaprogramming 
          -where two plain cases would                                   
       76 -do. Every concept a version adds (a class, an object, a flag o
          -r mode an abstraction                                         
       77 -branches on, a pattern) is listed next to its counts.         
       86 +**Decisions lead; Test and Read are guardrails.** A version be
          +ats V0 when:

       79 -## Three lists                                                
       88 +- it brings at least one decision from several places to one, 
          +or removes lockstep places,                                   
       89 +  or removes at least 2 places in total; or, on Test alone, it
          + moves at least 5 cases to                                    
       90 +  a setup of about 1 or less;                                 
       91 +- it adds at most one file to Read for each decision it brings
          + to one place;                                                
       92 +- and it is no worse on Test beyond noise.                    
       93  
       81 -Every version ends on one list.

          +eported as one.                                               
       95  
       83 -- ✅ **Apply.** It wins. It keeps the intended behavior, verif
          -iably. It touches existing                                    
       84 -  code only where the diff needs it, and then states why in on
          -e sentence ("the diff                                         
       85 -  needed X because existing code does, lacks or is shaped as Y
          -"), with existing churn at                                    
       86 -  most about twice the lines it saves. It touches no sensitive
          - code, and the measured                                       
       87 -  check in step 5 confirmed the win. Applied without asking.  
       88 -- 🛠️ **Your call.** It is worth it, but not safe to apply unas
          -ked: it changes intended                                      
       89 -  behavior, reaches further into existing code, touches sensit
          -ive code, adds a dependency                                   
       90 -  or a schema change, introduces a way of doing things this re
          -po doesn't use yet, or wins                                   
       91 -  some scenarios and loses others. Changes like these are welc
          -ome here when the counts                                      
       92 -  make them well worth it. Each one states its win, its cost, 
          -its risk and your                                             
       93 -  recommendation.                                             
       94 -- 🚫 **Not pursued.** No win beyond noise, worse on a lens, a 
          -prediction that failed,                                       
       95 -  evidence that didn't hold, or it contradicts a decision. One
          - line each.                                                   
       96 +**Counts can be gamed.** Shorter but denser doesn't count: pac
          +ked lines, long call chains,                                  
       97 +clever tricks, metaprogramming where two plain cases would do.
          + Every concept a version                                      
       98 +adds (a class, an object, a flag or mode an abstraction branch

       97 -## Sensitive code                                             
      101 +**No speculation.** A version must lower counts for decisions 
          +the code makes today. A                                       
      102 +seam, registry or strategy built for a variant that doesn't ex
          +ist yet wins nothing here.                                    
      103 +Future work enters only when the human names it (step 1); it i
          +s then counted as an extra                                    
      104 +row and marked as such.                                       
      105  
       99 -Sensitive code never goes on Apply, however small the edit: sm
          -all edits in sensitive code

          - One strong signal is
