      106 +## Three lists                                                
      107  
      103 -- **Money and access:** payments, prices, invoices, balances; 
          -authentication, sessions,                                     
      104 -  permissions, authorization checks; tokens and secrets.      
      105 -- **Persistent data:** writes to the database, migrations, bac
          -kground jobs that change                                      
      106 -  records, imports and exports, deletion.                     
      107 -- **Contracts:** public API endpoints and response shapes, web
          -hooks, events other systems                                   
      108 -  consume, URLs people bookmark, file formats.                
      109 -- **Input from outside:** validation, sanitizing, limits on wh
          -at a client may send.                                         
      110 -- **Concurrency:** locks, transactions, retries, caching and i
          -ts invalidation.                                              
      111 -- **History:** a hot spot in the cochange output (many commits
          -, many partners), or code                                     
      112 -  with a history of bug-fix commits (`git log --grep` for fix,
          - bug or revert on the file).                                  
      113 -- **No safety net:** code no test covers.                     
      114 -- **The code says so:** comments warning about it, or unusual 
          -care like extra logging,                                      
      115 -  defensive checks or feature flags.                          
      108 +Every version ends on one list.                               
      109  
      110 +- ✅ **Recommended.** It wins on the rules above, keeps the in
          +tended behavior, and fits                                     
      111 +  the code without a large existing churn (at most about twice
          + the places it saves,                                         
      112 +  in lines).                                                  
      113 +- 🛠️ **Trade-off.** Worth considering, but the human has to we
          +igh it: the lenses disagree,                                  
      114 +  it changes intended behavior, reaches far into existing code
          +, touches sensitive code,                                     
      115 +  adds a dependency or a schema change, or introduces a way of
          + doing things this repo                                       
      116 +  doesn't use yet. A new way is not a reason to drop a version
          +: count the siblings that                                     
      117 +  would then do it differently, and let the human decide.     
      118 +- 🚫 **Not pursued.** No win beyond noise, worse on a lens, a 
          +prediction that failed,                                       
      119 +  evidence that didn't hold, or it contradicts a choice the hu
          +man already made. One line                                    
      120 +  each.

       2  
       3  Starting points for versions, not answers. Each move states wha

       4  costs. A move is only as good as its counts: a version built fr
          om it must win on the
       5 -lenses, or the prediction failed for this code. The repo's own 
         -way of doing a thing comes                                     
       6 -before any move here.

         +s good or bad in itself.

       7  | Move | Predicts | Costs | Signal in the code |
       8  |---|---|---|---|
      10 -| Put a rule with the data it reads | fewer units for "change t
         -he rule"; unit tests instead of request or TestBed tests | can 
         -bloat the data owner; watch Read | most of the code's reads go 
         -to one other object |

         - if the home is new | the same condition or constant in 2+ plac

         -; smaller test setup | +1 unit, maybe +1 Read file | methods fa
         -ll into groups that share no fields, or the unit is described w
         -ith "and" |

         -the merged unit grows | deleting the unit moves its complexity 
         -rather than spreading it to callers |                          
      14 -| One state value instead of several flags | fewer cases to tes
         -t; checks for invalid combinations disappear | a schema change 
         -if the flags are stored | several booleans or optional fields c
         -hecked together |

         -ters passed along together |

         - variant" touches one place | +1 concept per variant class | th
         -e same `if` or `case` on the same value in 2+ places |

         -thout the outside system | +1 interface | only pays off with tw
         -o real implementations, production and test; with one it is ind

✻ Worked for 4s · done Saturday 2:22 PM

❯ what does this mean? s common gap is never comparing test setup               

● It was shorthand for this: in all three evals, plain Claude without the skill
  never compared the designs on how much a test has to set up.

  Concretely, when the baseline suggested moving a rule, it wrote something like
  "move this into the model, it's easier to test there." It never said what
  that means in numbers. It didn't say what a test of that rule has to build
  today versus after the move. That's the claim you can't check, and the kind of
  reasoning you wanted to get away from.

  With the skill, the same suggestion comes with the comparison:

  ┌──────────────────────┬──────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                      │             Test of "unsave a story"             │
  ├──────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Today (logic in the  │ request spec: create user, create story, create  │
  │ controller)          │ saved record, log in, send request = setup 5     │
  ├──────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ After V1 (logic on   │ model spec: create user, create story, create    │
  │ SavedStory)          │ saved record = setup 3                           │
  └──────────────────────┴──────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  That's the "setup 5→3" in the lobsters report above.

  Setup is the lens you asked for: it makes the difference between a
  well-isolated rule and one buried in a controller or component measurable. In
  Angular the gap would be bigger, for example a TestBed with 5 providers and
  mocks against a plain function with 0. "Never comparing test setup" means the
  baseline left that whole lens out; it only said "easier to test" in words.

  - **Expectation 1:** none of the report's multi-place decisions was edited at

    - `/saved.rss?token=` ignores the token. Fixed upstream the same day by
  359e63a0, which is V3's first step.

  and 9a11ab86.

  though it is harmless.
  - One claim is overstated: V2 saves a net 14 lines, not 16.
  - The "pinning" spec was written against the new API (`for_user`,
  `unsave_story_for_user`), so it couldn't have run against the code as written.
  It was never run.
  - At 33 non-empty lines, the report is over the 30-line scannability limit.

  **Suggestions for the evals**
  - Decide whether a refactor that moves one of the counted places counts as a
  decision change. Graders will disagree on 06044acb otherwise.
  - Add a separate check for "a recommended move matches what maintainers later
  did".

  - Add a check that a pinning spec actually pins the behavior from before the
  refactor.

● The lobsters grade is in, and it isn't the clean answer I'd hoped for.
  Iteration 2 passes all four method checks and fails both checks against real 
  history:

  - Decision table vs reality: failed. The decisions it showed as spread (the
    save lookup in 5 places, the hide/save subquery in 2) were never changed as
    decisions later. Upstream only refactored one place at a time. The one rule
    the maintainers really had to edit in several places, the tag filter in
    0809bfea, was counted as sitting in one place.
  - Design verdict: failed. What it applied (the for_user scope and the merged
    JavaScript toggle) mostly missed what history touched. Nobody ever edited
    the JavaScript toggle again. Only the larger V4, which it left as a
    trade-off, would have folded together the code that was later refactored.

  A real defect the grader caught: the "pin-down" spec was written against the
  new API. It couldn't have run against the code as it was, so it pinned
  nothing. That breaks the cleanup safety the skill promises when it applies
  changes.

  So on lobsters, iteration 2 analyses more than iteration 1 but doesn't predict
  history better. There's a deeper point here. Counting places is
  non-speculative, but a decision in 5 places only costs something if that 
  decision ever changes. In lobsters, "which saves belong to a user" never
  changed. That's the tension between your "robust for whatever comes" goal and
  the metric: places measure exposure, not how likely change is.

  What I'd consider for iteration 3:

  1. Weigh decisions by change history. A decision whose places git shows
     changing together often gets weighted up; one that never changes gets
     weighted down. It's evidence, not speculation. The co-change script already
     has the data.
  2. Show at most 3 versions, to get the report back under 30 lines.
  3. Pin-down tests must run green against the code before the change. That's a
     check the skill can do itself.

  Maybe and ghostfolio are still running. One eval is a single data point, and
  lobsters was the hardest case in iteration 1 too. I'll wait for all three
  before changing anything.

  The headline is 71% against 50%. The last row fails for every run because the

  analysis-first default; that's my test's fault. Without it, the skill scores
  81% and the baseline 57%.

  Is it better than iteration 1? Better at what you asked for, not better at 
  predicting history.

  - Maybe and ghostfolio were strong. In maybe, the maintainers rewrote exactly
    the 3 places the report counted, and built its V2. In ghostfolio, the shared
    tags selector it applied is the component the maintainers built 6 months

    places was counted as one place.
  - The baseline is good at seeing duplication and matched history 3 of 3 on the
    design verdict. The skill's advantage is making the claims countable and
    comparable, not finding things the baseline misses.

  The two lessons for iteration 3:

  1. Places measure exposure, not how likely change is. A decision in 5 places
     that never changes costs nothing. Git history shows which decisions
     actually change together, so weighting decisions by that is evidence, not
     speculation. This would have helped in lobsters.
  2. The churn guard is too cautious. In maybe, it talked down the version
     history later confirmed. A decision that really changes justifies more
     churn.

  Also to fix:
  - Pin-down tests must pass on the code before the change. The lobsters one was
    written against the new code.
  - Show at most 3 versions, to keep the report under 30 lines.
  - Evals: remove the "apply" wording from the test prompt, and tighten the
    decision-table assertion so a later commit counts only if it changed what a
    decision does.

  1. Commit iteration 2's skill now (it's uncommitted in master-terminal), or
     wait until after iteration 3? I'd commit: it's closer to what you want than

✻ Cooked for 28m 16s · done Saturday 2:33 PM

❯ what do you mean by predict history?                                          

● It's what the backtest measures. Each test run saw a repo frozen at a past 
  commit, as if the feature had just been written, with no knowledge of what
  came next. Then we checked its report against what the maintainers actually 
  did in the 18 months after.

  "Predicting history" means: did the report point at the code that really
  caused work later?

  Concretely, for maybe:

  - The skill's report said: "how balances become a series" is decided in 3
    places (three near-identical *_series methods), and V2 would merge them into
    one.
  - What really happened: four days later the maintainers had to change that
    logic, and they merged exactly those 3 methods into one (commit c60ddaec).

  That's a hit. The report flagged the hassle before it happened, and its fix
  was the one the team ended up needing.

  For lobsters:

  - The skill's report said: "which saves belong to a user" sits in 5 places, so
    that's the risk.
  - What really happened: nobody ever changed that rule. The work that did come
    was in other places, like the tag filter, which the report counted as fine.

  That's a miss. The report's warning was technically true (5 places), but it
  pointed at a cost that never came due.

  This is the only way to check whether "fewer places per decision" really means
  "less hassle later". Otherwise we'd just be trusting the metric. So far:

  - Hits: 2 of 3 commits.
  - Misses: lobsters, because a spread decision only hurts if it ever changes.

  That's why I suggested using git history to see which decisions actually
  change.

✻ Churned for 10s · done Saturday 2:35 PM

❯ ok anything to improveo n the skill from this turn?                           

● Yes. Iteration 2 points at five concrete changes, ordered by how much evidence
  is behind them:

  1. Weigh decisions by how often they really change. (lobsters miss) For each
     decision spread over several places, check git history: how often did those
     places change, and did they change together? The table gets a 🔥 marker or
     a "changed 6× in history" note. A spread decision that history shows
     changing is a priority. One that never changed is shown as low priority,
     and a version that only fixes those doesn't get recommended unless it's
     cheap. This is evidence, not speculation, and it would have turned the
     lobsters miss into a "nothing worth doing" verdict.
  2. Let a hot decision justify more churn. (maybe, where it talked down the 
     right version) Today, churn may be at most about twice the lines saved. For
     a decision with a history of change, allow more, around 5×. The cost comes
     due anyway; history shows it does.
  3. Find every place of a decision, across the whole codebase. (lobsters tag 
     filter) The tag filter was counted as 1 place, but its places sat in
     existing queries outside the diff's files. The decision list should grep
     the codebase for each rule's other places, not only the files in the diff.
  4. Pin-down tests must pass against the code before the change. (lobsters) The
     lobsters spec was written against the new API, so it pinned nothing. Rule:
     write it first, check it runs green on the old code where tests can run,
     and only then refactor.
  5. Keep the report short and readable.
     - Show at most 3 versions, merging or dropping the weaker ones. Lobsters
       showed 5 and went over 30 lines.
     - Label the units: "4.7" means nothing until it reads "setup 4.7/case".

  I'd leave the rest alone:

  - The three lenses held up. Test setup was the clearest gain over plain
    Claude.
  - Analysis-first held up. It only applied changes because my test prompt asked
    it to.
  - Principles as predictions stay. They worked 2 of 3 times, and the one miss
    was a single unchecked rejection.

  Should I apply these five to the skill and commit it to master-terminal? After
  that, an iteration 3 run (about $8) would show whether the history weighting
  fixes lobsters without breaking maybe and ghostfolio.

✻ Sautéed for 17s · done Saturday 2:36 PM

❯ explain in simple terms what 1-5 would change                                 

● Here's what each change does in practice:

  1. Care about what actually changes. Today the skill says "this rule is copied
     in 5 places, that's risky". But if nobody has touched that rule in years,
     the copies cost nothing. After the change, it checks git history first.
     Copies that keep getting edited together are flagged as real hassle; copies
     that never changed are only noted, not pushed.
  2. Allow a bigger cleanup when it's clearly needed. Today the skill rejects a
     refactor if it changes too much existing code compared with what it saves.
     That's too cautious when the messy part changes all the time: in maybe it
     rejected exactly the cleanup the team did four days later. After the
     change, a part that history shows changing often may get a bigger cleanup.
  3. Look everywhere, not just at the new code. Today it mostly counts copies
     inside the files you changed, so it missed a rule that was also copied in
     older files. After the change, it searches the whole codebase for each
     rule.
  4. Make the safety tests actually test the old code. Before refactoring, it
     writes tests that capture how the code behaves right now, so the refactor
     can't silently break anything. In lobsters it wrote those tests against the
     new code, so they protected nothing. After the change, the tests are
     written first and checked to pass on the old code.
  5. A shorter report. At most 3 alternatives instead of 5, and numbers with
     their meaning attached ("setup 4.7 per test" instead of a bare "4.7").

  In one sentence: the skill would look at real history to judge which messes
  matter, search more widely to find them, refactor more boldly where it pays
  off, test its refactors properly, and say it in fewer lines.

✻ Cogitated for 3s · done Saturday 7:12 PM

❯ what is  Test cost counts setup? why does it fail?                            

● "Test cost counts setup" is the check that the report compares designs on how 
  much a test has to build before it can test anything: the records, the
  logged-in user, the mocks, the TestBed providers. It's the lens you asked for,
  where Angular's TestBed makes isolation "very palpable".

  What the assertion requires: for each design, the report states that setup,
  with its unit named, so you know what the number means.

  Why it failed: the skill did count setup, but the report presented it badly in
  two ways.

  1. The number has no meaning attached. The lobsters report shows:
  Tests (9 cases)   ~92 lines · setup 3.2/case   ~90 lines · 3.2   ~75 lines · 
  3.1
     "3.2" of what? Records? Mocks? The report never says, so you can't tell
     what you'd actually build in a test.
  2. It's often the same number in every column. In maybe, every design showed
     "setup 3.0/case". The sub-agents had counted different numbers per design,
     but the final report blended them into one figure. So the column that
     should show "design B is easier to test" showed no difference at all.

  So the counting worked and the reporting threw it away. That's why the grader
  failed it in all three evals.

  What I changed since (committed, but not yet tested):

  - The unit goes in the number: 3.4 setup items/case.
  - A legend line below the table says what's counted, like setup items: 
    records, login, request.
  - Each design shows its own count, never one blended figure.

  So the next report would read like this:

  Tests (7 cases)   140 lines · 3.4/case   90 lines · 1.6/case
  setup items per case: records, login, request

  Now you can see that design B's tests need about half as much setup, and what
  that setup is.

✻ Cooked for 10s · done Saturday 7:34 PM

❯ do you think counting lines of code for test and setup would be better?       

● The report already shows both (140 lines · 3.4/case). The real question is
  which one to lead with, and I'd keep the setup items as the main number, with
  lines second:

  Intuitive
  Lines of test and setup: Very: "this test is 40 lines"
  Setup items (collaborators): Needs the legend line
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Robust to style
  Lines of test and setup: Weak. A shared factory or beforeEach helper hides 20
  lines; Angular boilerplate (imports, TestBed.configureTestingModule,
  fixture.detectChanges()) inflates them without the design being worse.
  Setup items (collaborators): Strong. A mocked HttpClient counts as 1 whether
  it
  takes 2 lines or 10.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Measures what you care about
  Lines of test and setup: Typing effort
  Setup items (collaborators): How many things the code drags in. That's
  coupling, the same thing the Decisions lens measures from the other side.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Reliable for agents
  Lines of test and setup: Poorly: they're estimating lines of tests that don't
  exist yet, which is guesswork
  Setup items (collaborators): Well: they only have to list what a test would
  need, which they can read off the code

  An example where they disagree: an Angular component tested with a shared
  setupTestBed() helper is only 15 lines, but it still needs 5 providers and 2
  mocks. The lines say "cheap"; the items say "this component knows too much".
  The items are right, and that's the robustness signal you want.

  Where lines add something: they catch a test that needs few collaborators but
  very complex arrangement, like building a big nested record graph, which items
  undercount. That's why I'd keep them as the second number rather than drop
  them.

  If you'd rather lead with lines, one improvement would help: count setup lines
  only (everything before the first assertion) instead of whole test lines.
  That measures arrangement effort and ignores assertions, which don't say
  anything about design.

  My recommendation is to keep it as it is now: items first, lines second, with
  a legend. Want me to change anything there?

     review of a finished change; too much to run on every small edit.

  5. Your own code. Three open-source repos aren't your Rails and Angular
     codebase at work. The real test is running it on 3–4 of your recent diffs

     through.

❯ ok can we try the following: find commits in a solid shape, then make them    
  into a bad shape anda compare the shape the skill would suggest vs the actual 
  shape and judge the results, find around 5 commits, then tell me how close    
  the skills was the the actual result with downs/upsides, or maybe even        
  better, than see if we can improve smth further                               

● Yes, that's a good test, and it answers something the backtests couldn't. The
  backtests asked "did the skill predict what happened next?", which is noisy,
  because the future depends on what the business wanted. Your setup asks a
  cleaner question: given code in a bad shape, does the skill find its way back 
  to a known good shape? The original commit is the answer key.

  Plan:

  1. Pick about 5 commits in solid shape: Rails and Angular features with clear
     homes for their rules. I'm adding 37signals' Campfire next to maybe and
     ghostfolio, since it's about as clean as Rails code gets.
  2. Make each one worse. A separate agent rewrites each commit into a worse but
     working version, the way a hurried developer or a careless agent would: a
     rule copied into the controller and the view, a component that fetches and
     renders, flags instead of one state. It keeps behaviour identical, leaves
     no hint comments, and writes a hidden log of what it degraded.
  3. Run shape-code and plain Claude on each degraded change, sequentially, to
     stay under the usage limit.
  4. Grade each degradation:
     - Found: did it find the degradation?
     - Restored: did its recommended shape restore the original?
     - Extras: were its other suggestions improvements or noise?
  5. Then improve the skill from what it missed.

  Cost: about $25 of usage, with the five eval pairs run one at a time.

  Starting with cloning Campfire and finding candidate commits:

✻ Baked for 3m 32s · done 12:37 PM

❯ one question does the skill sum up total setup of the diff with the test      
  counts of the required setup? e.g. 5 integration times 4 setups and 4 unit    
  tests times 2 setup                                                           

● Not quite. Today it reports the average per case, not the total. Your example
  works out like this:

  - Total: 5 integration × 4 + 4 unit × 2 = 28 setup items
  - What the report shows: 28 / 9 cases = 3.1/case

  The total is only implicit, and the split by level is lost entirely.

  You're right that this hides something. These two designs both average about
  3.1:

  ┌─────┬────────────────────────────────┬───────┬──────────┐
  │     │             Cases              │ Setup │ Average  │
  ├─────┼────────────────────────────────┼───────┼──────────┤
  │ A   │ 5 integration × 4 + 4 unit × 2 │ 28    │ 3.1/case │
  ├─────┼────────────────────────────────┼───────┼──────────┤
  │ B   │ 9 cases × ~3 each              │ 28    │ 3.1/case │
  └─────┴────────────────────────────────┴───────┴──────────┘

  But they're not the same. A has a clear split, with most cases cheap and a few
  heavy wiring tests; B is uniformly medium. And if one design needs more cases
  than the other, an average can even make the worse design look cheaper.

  What I'd show instead, total plus the mix in one compact cell:

  Tests   28 setup (5 int × 4, 4 unit × 2) · 120 lines

  That tells you at a glance:

  - total burden: 28
  - where it sits: the integration tests carry 20 of the 28
  - what a better shape would change: move 3 of those cases to unit level and
    the total drops to 22

  That's the test pyramid as a number.

  It's a small change to the report format and the counting manual. Should I
  make it now? It wouldn't affect the degradation runs about to start, since
  they use the current skill copy, so I'd apply it after this test, together
  with whatever the test turns up.

✻ Crunched for 13s · done 12:39 PM

❯ would it be worht it? i think it might be good wdyt?                          

● Yes, I think it's worth it:

  - It's more honest. Versions often need different numbers of cases; one design
    may make an edge case impossible. An average hides that; a total doesn't.
  - It shows the pyramid as a number. "20 of 28 setup items sit in 5 integration
    tests" tells you exactly where a better shape pays off. That's the "how
    palpable is testing here" question you raised for Angular.
  - It costs almost nothing: one cell in the report, one paragraph in the
    counting manual.

  The only downside is a slightly wider cell, which 28 setup (5 int × 4, 4 unit 
  × 2) keeps acceptable.

  The degradation runs haven't been built yet; the skill gets copied in only
  after the degraders finish. So if I change it now, this test covers the new
  format too. Doing that:
